Full text

Abstract

Meta-analytic structural equation modeling (MASEM) is increasingly used in psychological research to evaluate multivariate models, such as path or factor models, across multiple primary studies. Several approaches to MASEM have been proposed that differ in whether and where they represent between-study heterogeneity, most notably the fixed-effect, correlation-based, and parameter-based approaches. This study investigates under which conditions these approaches target equivalent or divergent structural parameter estimates.

We derive analytical conditions for equivalence and divergence and examine them in three simulation studies comparing one-stage implementations under parameter-based data-generating mechanisms. The results show that the approaches produce approximately equivalent estimates when the model-implied correlations depend linearly on the heterogeneous parameters. However, multidimensional, correlated parameter heterogeneity produces target discrepancies between the parameter-based and the other two approaches.

These discrepancies increase with the magnitude of between-study heterogeneity. Nonlinear parameter transformations introduce additional discrepancies, even under unidimensional heterogeneity. Under the standard implementations used, almost no differences emerged between the fixed-effect and the correlation-based population targets. Two applications to psychological research further illustrate the model comparison. Overall, the findings show that the location of random effects can affect the target of structural parameter estimation in MASEM even when the study-specific structural models are correctly specified. It follows that the choice among the approaches constitutes a substantive decision for researchers between distinct statistical estimands: the mean of study-specific structural parameters (parameter-based MASEM) and a common parameter vector that best represents the mean correlations across studies (fixed-effect and correlation-based MASEM).

1 Introduction

Meta-analytic structural equation modeling (MASEM) combines the statistical approaches of meta-analysis and structural equation modeling (SEM) to evaluate multivariate theoretical models across multiple primary studies (e.g., Cheung, 2015a). It has become an increasingly popular tool in psychological research for synthesizing findings on multivariate interrelations, with research applications mainly focusing on the evaluation of path models testing multivariate psychological theories (e.g., Hagger et al., 2018; Ho et al., 2022; Li et al., 2023; Möller et al., 2020; Scherer et al., 2019; Vasconcellos et al., 2025), as well as the examination of latent factor structures across studies (e.g., Gnambs & Staufenbiel, 2018; Kindred et al., 2026; Koğar & Koğar, 2024; Naragon-Gainey et al., 2017; Schroeders et al., 2022; Strunk et al., 2021; Yeung et al., 2020).

In psychological research, primary studies addressing the same theoretical model or research question are rarely exact replications. They typically differ in sample characteristics, measurement instruments, operationalizations, and study designs. As a consequence, substantial between-study heterogeneity beyond sampling error is typically found in meta-analyses (e.g., Hunter & Schmidt, 2004). In psychological research, meta-analytic methods are therefore essential not only for aggregating findings but also for examining the generalizability of theoretical models and identifying conditions under which they hold or do not hold (Hunter & Schmidt, 2004; Schmidt, 2015). Accordingly, a central methodological question in MASEM is how to represent this between-study heterogeneity-and where to locate it within the statistical model.

Several approaches to MASEM have been proposed that account for heterogeneity at different levels of the model (e.g., Cheung & Cheung, 2016; Hedges, 2016; Ke et al., 2025; Oort & Jak, 2016; Scherer & Campos, 2025; van Zundert & Miočević, 2020). For example, fixed-effect MASEM attributes observed differences among the sample correlations entirely to sampling error (e.g., Cheung, 2014; Hafdahl, 2008; Jak & Cheung, 2018). In contrast, random-effects approaches to MASEM allow for additional between-study heterogeneity (e.g., Cheung & Cheung, 2016; Ke et al., 2025). More concretely, in the correlation-based approach, random effects are introduced at the level of the correlations, such that population correlations may vary across studies around a mean correlation structure (see, e.g., Cheung & Cheung, 2016; Jak & Cheung, 2020). In the parameter-based approach, random effects are instead introduced at the level of the structural parameters, such that study-specific parameter vectors vary around a vector of mean structural parameters (see, e.g., Cheung & Cheung, 2016; Ke et al., 2019; Lohmann et al., 2026a).

Although the differences among these approaches have received recurring attention in the MASEM literature (e.g., Cheung & Cheung, 2016; Ke et al., 2025), the conditions under which they target equivalent or divergent SEM parameters have-to the best of our knowledge-not yet been analyzed systematically. In particular, it remains unclear whether and when locating between-study heterogeneity at the level of the correlations rather than the structural parameters affects estimates of target SEM parameters. Previous work has therefore called for further research comparing these approaches (e.g., Jak & Cheung, 2020; Ke et al., 2025). The present study addresses this gap by deriving theoretical conditions under which the approaches can be expected to yield equivalent or divergent SEM target parameters and by examining these expectations through simulation studies and empirical applications.

The remainder of this article is organized as follows. We first introduce the fixed-effect, correlation-based, and parameter-based approaches to MASEM. Building on their statistical formulations, we derive conditions under which the approaches can be expected to yield asymptotically equivalent SEM parameter estimates and conditions under which their targets may diverge. We then examine these expectations in three simulation studies-using both path models and confirmatory factor analysis models-in which the data-generating process follows a parameter-based model. Next, we illustrate differences among the approaches using two empirical examples from psychological research. Finally, we discuss the implications of our findings and directions for future research.

1.1 Meta-Analytic Structural Equation Modeling

In a multivariate meta-analysis, each primary study s = 1, 2, . . . , S contributes a vector of k potentially correlated effect-size estimates and a corresponding k × k sampling covariance matrix. By jointly modeling multiple related effect sizes, multivariate meta-analytic methods account for their statistical dependencies and can improve statistical efficiency and inference compared with conducting separate univariate meta-analyses (e.g., Jackson et al., 2011; Riley et al., 2007). MASEM is a specific type of multivariate meta-analysis in which the effect-size vector contains pairwise sample correlations, denoted by r s , with corresponding sampling covariance matrix V s (Cheung, 2014 (Cheung, , 2015a)). A SEM relates these correlations to a model-implied correlation vector ρ(θ), where θ contains SEM parameters such as factor loadings or path coefficients. Thus, MASEM uses the study-specific correlation vectors and their sampling covariance matrices to estimate structural parameters across primary studies.

Accordingly, the SEM is typically formulated as a correlation structure model (Cudeck, 1989; Yuan & Zhang, 2025) in MASEM, with the model-implied variances of the observed variables constrained to one (e.g., Jak & Cheung, 2020; Ke et al., 2019). The model-implied vector of correlations is then given by where vechs(•) stacks the unique off-diagonal elements of a symmetric matrix.

Here, Λ = Λ(θ) is the matrix relating the observed variables to the latent or structural variables, whereas B = B(θ) contains the directed relations among the structural variables and I is the identity matrix of the same dimension as B. The matrix Ψ = Ψ(θ) contains the variances and covariances of the structural disturbances, including the variances and covariances of exogenous structural variables, and Ω = Ω(θ) contains residual variances and covariances at the level of the observed variables. The vector θ collects the underlying freely estimated SEM parameters. The mapping ρ(θ) is generally nonlinear in θ, because the model-implied correlations arise from products and inversions of matrices whose elements depend on θ. Writing the model matrices as functions of θ (e.g., B(θ)) makes explicit that their freely varying elements may either correspond directly to elements of θ or be obtained through nonlinear transformations, for example by exponentiating unconstrained parameters to ensure positive variances or by applying the inverse Fisher z transformation to map unconstrained parameters to correlations within (-1, 1).

The following subsections introduce the fixed-effect, correlation-based, and parameter-based approaches to MASEM. These approaches differ primarily in whether and at what level between-study heterogeneity is represented. A separate distinction in MASEM concerns the choice between one-stage and two-stage estimation. Historically, MASEM has predominantly relied on two-stage estimation procedures (e.g., Cheung, 2015a; Cheung & Cheung, 2016). Two-stage procedures separate the meta-analytic synthesis from the estimation of the structural model, whereas one-stage procedures jointly estimate the meta-analytic and structural components by fitting the model directly to the study-specific correlation vectors and their sampling covariance matrices (e.g., Cheung, 2015a; Jak & Cheung, 2020; Ke et al., 2019). One-stage procedures have received increasing attention in recent years, in part because they can directly accommodate incomplete correlation vectors, allow study-level moderators to predict structural parameters within the same model, and may yield more efficient parameter estimates when the model is correctly specified (Jak & Cheung, 2020; Ke et al., 2025; Lohmann et al., 2026a). The present study focuses on these one-stage approaches.

1.1.1 Fixed-Effect Meta-Analytic Structural Equation Modeling

In the fixed-effect approach to MASEM, all observed between-study variation in the sample correlations is attributed to sampling error. Accordingly, the vector of sample correlations r s in study s is modeled as where ρ(θ FE ) is the vector of correlations implied by the common structural parameter vector θ FE , and e s is a vector of study-specific sampling errors following a multivariate normal distribution,

It follows that the model-implied marginal mean correlation vector under the fixed-effect approach is

Thus, θ FE parameterizes the correlation vector that the fixed-effect model uses to represent the marginal mean correlation structure across studies.

In MASEM, V s is typically replaced by a precomputed estimate ˆ︂ V s , which is subsequently treated as known (see, e.g., Cheung & Chan, 2004; Olkin & Siotani, 1976).

Under the multivariate normal likelihood, the fixed effect approach weights deviations of the study-specific correlation vectors from the common structure by the estimated sampling precision matrices ˆ︂ V -1 s (see, e.g., Cheung & Chan, 2005; Yuan & Kano, 2018).

Because the fixed-effect approach includes no between-study covariance matrix, its weighting reflects sampling uncertainty alone.

1.1.2 Correlation-Based Meta-Analytic Structural Equation Modeling

To accommodate between-study variation beyond sampling error, random-effects approaches allow selected population quantities to vary across studies. In the correlation-based approach, random effects are introduced directly at the level of the study-specific population correlations (e.g., Becker, 1992; Cheung, 2015a; Cheung & Cheung, 2016; Ke et al., 2025). Specifically, the observed correlation vector is modeled as with u ρs ∼ N (0, T ρ ) and e s ∼ N (0, V s ).

Here, u ρs represents the study-specific deviation from the model-implied mean correlation vector ρ(θ CB ), and T ρ is the corresponding between-study covariance matrix. The random effects u ρs are assumed to be independent of the sampling errors e s.

The model-implied marginal mean correlation vector under the correlation-based approach is therefore

Thus, θ CB parameterizes the model-implied marginal mean correlation structure across studies, whereas T ρ describes the between-study heterogeneity around this mean structure.

As described for the fixed-effect approach, V s is replaced by its precomputed estimate ˆ︂ V s . Given T ρ , deviations from the model-implied marginal mean are weighted by the precision matrix ( ˆ︂ V s + T ρ ) -1 (e.g., Becker, 1992; Cheung, 2015a; Yuan & Kano, 2018).

The correlation-based weighting therefore accounts for both sampling uncertainty and the estimated between-study covariance structure, whereas the fixed-effect weighting is based on sampling uncertainty alone.

1.1.3 Parameter-Based Meta-Analytic Structural Equation Modeling

In the parameter-based approach, by contrast, random effects are introduced at the level of the structural parameters. Let the study-specific structural parameter vector be given by where θ PB denotes the population-level mean structural parameter vector.

The observed correlation vector is then modeled as with

Thus, u θs represents the study-specific deviation from the mean structural parameter vector θ PB , and T θ is the corresponding between-study covariance matrix.

The model-implied marginal mean correlation vector under the parameter-based approach is given by

Importantly, because ρ(•) is generally nonlinear (cf. Equation 1), the marginal mean correlation vector in Equation 11 does not necessarily equal ρ(θ PB )-the correlation vector that the fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches target as the marginal mean correlation structure. The conditions under which the two coincide are examined in Section 1.2.

1.1.4 Target Estimands

Previous comparisons of the correlation-based, fixed-effect, and parameter-based approaches have primarily focused on the different heterogeneity components of the models (Ke et al., 2025) or on practical considerations, such as missing correlations or moderator analyses, when using two-stage implementations of the different models (Cheung & Cheung, 2016; Lohmann et al., 2026a). However, the structural parameters themselves have received surprisingly little attention so far, even though the three approaches target distinct statistical estimands. The parameter-based approach estimates the mean of the study-specific structural parameters and thus addresses the research question: What are the average structural parameters across the population of studies? The fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches instead estimate the structural parameters that best reproduce the marginal mean correlation structure and thus address the research question:

What single set of structural parameters best accounts for the average pattern of correlations across studies? In addition, although the fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches address the same question, they may arrive at different answers because they weight the study-specific contributions differently (Forte & Tipton, 2026; Hafdahl, 2008).

Thus, it has rarely been made explicit that the location of random effect can affect what the approaches estimate as the structural parameters, even when using the same structural model and when applied to the same data. Because averaging structural parameters and parameterizing the average correlation structure need not yield the same result, these questions can have different answers even when the study-specific structural models are correctly specified. The central question addressed in the current article is under which conditions the answers coincide and under which conditions they diverge.

1.2 Asymptotic Relationship Between the Three Approaches

We now examine conditions under which the three approaches, although targeting different statistical estimands, converge to the same structural parameter vector and conditions under which their targets may diverge. In the present analyses, we use a parameter-based data-generating mechanism as the reference setting to study the consequences of locating heterogeneity at different levels. This choice serves as an analytical device and does not imply that parameter-based MASEM is generally preferable or that heterogeneity necessarily originates at the parameter level.

Our examination is conducted under the following conditions. First, it is assumed that the same structural model is correctly specified within every primary study, so that each study-specific population correlation vector equals ρ(θ s ). The analysis therefore does not concern structural model misspecification within primary studies but focuses on discrepancies that may arise when-instead of averaging structural parameters across correctly specified study-specific models (parameter-based approach)-the average correlation structure is represented through a common structural parameter vector (fixed-effect and correlation-based approach). Second, primary studies are independent, sampling errors are unbiased, and there are no systematic associations of sample size with the study-specific structural parameters. Furthermore, all results in the current section concern the asymptotic structural parameter targets of the three MASEM approaches as the number of primary studies tends to infinity.

1.2.1 Order of Averaging and the Mean-Correlation Discrepancy

In the fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches, the structural parameter vector is chosen to reproduce the marginal mean correlation structure (Equations 4 and 7). In the parameter-based approach, by contrast, θ PB represents the mean of the study-specific structural parameter vectors. A first step toward comparing the asymptotic targets of the three approaches is therefore to examine whether the mean parameter vector also reproduces the marginal mean correlation structure (cf. Equation 11)-that is, whether

A sufficient condition is that the mapping ρ(•) is linear in the heterogeneous parameters.

Nonlinearity makes a difference between the two sides possible but does not imply that they must differ in every model or for every random-effects structure. We define the mean-correlation discrepancy as where m ρ = E[ρ(θ s )] denotes the marginal mean correlation vector under the parameter-based data-generating mechanism (cf.

Equation 11). Whether and how a nonzero δ ρ translates into differences between the asymptotic structural parameter targets θ FE , θ CB , and θ PB , is examined after describing its leading sources.

The leading sources of δ ρ can be described using a second-order Taylor approximation. Let ρ j (θ) denote the jth element of the model-implied correlation vector and assume that this function is twice differentiable in a neighborhood of θ PB.

Expanding ρ j (θ i ) around θ PB gives where ∇ρ j (θ PB ) is the gradient vector containing the first derivatives of ρ j (θ), and H j (θ PB ) is its Hessian matrix containing the second derivatives.

Both are evaluated at the mean parameter vector θ PB . Thus, the study-specific model-implied correlation is decomposed intro three parts-the correlation at the mean parameters, a linear adjustment for the study's parameter deviation, and a quadratic term that captures curvature in the mapping. What remains is the quadratic term, which highlights that the mean-correlation discrepancy is determined by the interplay of two quantities: the curvature of the mapping ρ(•) at the mean parameters (captured by the Hessian H j ) and the magnitude and covariance structure of the parameter heterogeneity (captured by T θ ).

Taking expectations of Equation 14and using E(u θi ) = 0 yields

The first-order term vanishes because the parameter random effects are centered at zero. Equation 15shows that the mean-correlation discrepancy depends jointly on the local curvature of the mapping ρ(•) and on the magnitude and covariance structure of the parameter heterogeneity T θ . Because the discrepancy is determined by the elements of T θ , it is small when heterogeneity is small and grows with the square of the random-effect standard deviations. Moreover, note that nonlinearity does not necessarily produce a discrepancy: the relevant curvature terms may be zero, the corresponding parameters may not vary across studies, or different contributions may cancel. Concrete sources of curvature in common structural models are illustrated in the examples below.

1.2.2 Target Parameter Discrepancies

Under the parameter-based data-generating mechanism, the asymptotic target of the parameter-based approach is θ PB , that is, the parameter-based MASEM holds. The question is how the mean-correlation discrepancy δ ρ affects the asymptotic targets of the fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches-referred to jointly as the common-parameter approaches-relative to this reference. Let A ∈ {FE, CB} and let θ * A denote the asymptotic structural parameter target. Both common-parameter approaches seek the parameter vector that best reproduces the marginal mean correlation vector under their respective weighting (e.g., Cheung, 2015a; Yuan & Kano, 2018): where W A is the positive-definite limiting weight matrix.

For the fixed-effect approach, this weighting is based on the sampling covariance structure, whereas the correlation-based approach additionally incorporates the between-study covariance structure (cf. Section 1.1).

The derivation of the target discrepancy adapts an approach used by Kolenikov (2011), who derived parameter biases under structural misspecification in ordinary SEMs.

Here, the discrepancy arises not from an incorrect structural model but from representing the averaged correlation structure of heterogeneous studies through a single parameter vector. Let ∆ 0 denote the k × q Jacobian of the model-implied correlation vector with respect to the structural parameters, evaluated at θ PB , where k is the number of correlations in r s and q is the number of parameters in θ. A first-order Taylor expansion of

Substituting into the population criterion and solving the weighted least-squares problem yields

This result requires ∆ 0 to have full column rank. The matrix Q A translates the mean-correlation discrepancy into a structural parameter discrepancy through a weighted projection onto the space of correlation changes that the structural model can produce.

Because Q A is a linear mapping and δ ρ grows with the square of the random-effect standard deviations, the target discrepancy inherits this scaling.

1.2.3 Illustrations of Target Parameter Discrepancies

We illustrate the preceding results using two examples that highlight distinct sources of target discrepancies. The first concerns correlated heterogeneity in multiple factor loadings, whereas the second concerns heterogeneity in a single factor correlation modeled on the Fisher z scale.

Correlated Loading Heterogeneity in a Three-Indicator CFA. Consider a standardized one-factor model with three indicators, unit factor variance, and mutually uncorrelated indicator residuals. Let λ j,PB denote the mean loading of indicator j, and let its study-specific loading be given by λ js = λ j,PB + u λ j s , where the loading random effects are centered at zero. We denote their between-study covariances by τ jk = Cov(u λ j s , u λ k s ).

The study-specific model-implied correlation between indicators j and k is ρ jk,s = λ js λ ks.

Consequently, its marginal mean is

Thus, correlated loading heterogeneity shifts the marginal mean correlations away from the correlations implied by the mean loadings. In this case, the Taylor approximation in Equation 15is exact because the model-implied correlations are quadratic in the loadings.

Moreover, only covariances between loading random effects contribute to the discrepancy-the individual between-study loading variances do not enter the mean correlations.

The one-factor model with three indicators is just identified. Assuming positive loadings, the first loading can be recovered as λ 21 = ρ 12 ρ 13 ρ 23 . The asymptotic target of either common-parameter approach therefore satisfies

A first-order expansion in the between-study covariance terms, followed by a first-order expansion of the square root, yields

The three terms may reinforce or offset one another. If the loading random effects are mutually uncorrelated, all covariance terms are zero and the common-parameter target coincides with the mean loading despite the presence of loading heterogeneity. Conversely, positive covariances do not necessarily produce a positive target discrepancy because their effects depend on where they enter the identifying relation. Because the model is just identified, the weighting matrix does not affect the estimand and the fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches share the same asymptotic loading target.

Heterogeneity on the Fisher z Scale.

A target discrepancy may also arise from a nonlinear parameter transformation when only a single parameter varies across studies.

Fisher z transformations are widely used in meta-analytic settings to map correlations to an unbounded scale (e.g., Hafdahl, 2009). Consider a latent factor correlation parameterized as where ζ PB is the mean on the Fisher z scale and ϕ0 = tanh(ζ PB ) is the corresponding factor correlation (see also Igelmann et al., 2026).

All other structural parameters are assumed constant across studies. Although the model-implied indicator correlations are linear in ϕ s when the loadings are fixed, they are nonlinear in the heterogeneous parameter ζ s . A second-order Taylor approximation gives

The first expression gives the shift in the marginal mean factor correlation due to the The two examples illustrate distinct sources of target discrepancies: correlated heterogeneity across multiple parameters in the first and a nonlinear parameter transformation in the second. In both cases, the marginal mean correlation structure can be reproduced exactly by another parameter vector, so that the fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches share the same target. However, in the setting considered here (cf. Section 1.2), weighting-dependent differences between these two approaches can arise when exact reproduction is not possible, as discussed next.

1.2.4 Summary and Predictions

The preceding analysis showed how the asymptotic targets of the parameter-based approach and the common-parameter approaches (i.e., fixed-effect and correlation-based MASEM) may diverge under parameter-based data generation. Curvature in the mapping from structural parameters to model-implied correlations can produce a mean-correlation discrepancy that grows with the magnitude of parameter heterogeneity and that translates into structural parameter target differences through the local sensitivity of the model.

These mechanisms yield several qualitative predictions that the following simulation studies are designed to examine. First, correlated, multidimensional parameter heterogeneity should produce target discrepancies even in conventional structural models, whereas unidimensional heterogeneity need not. Second, nonlinear parameter transformations should introduce additional discrepancies and show discrepancies even under unidimensional heterogeneity. Third, target discrepancies between the parameter-based approach and the fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches should increase with the magnitude of parameter heterogeneity. Finally, because these predictions concern asymptotic targets, the corresponding discrepancies should persist as the number of primary studies increases.

1.2.5 Comparing the Fixed-Effect and Correlation-Based Approaches

The preceding analysis focused on conditions under which the parameter-based target may differ from the targets of the fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches. A separate question is whether the fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches may also differ from each other. Both approaches represent the marginal mean correlation structure through a common structural parameter vector but assign different weights to the study-specific contributions: the fixed-effect approach weights by ˆ︂ V -1 s , whereas the correlation-based approach weights by ( ˆ︂ V s + T ρ ) -1 (cf. Section 1.1). Previous research on univariate meta-analysis suggests that the asymptotic targets of fixed-effect and random-effects estimators coincide when the study-specific weights are independent of the population effect sizes (Forte & Tipton, 2026). A key determinant of this independence is the computation of the sampling covariance matrices ˆ︂ V s (Hafdahl, 2008): when they are derived from study-specific sample correlations, the weights depend on the study-specific population correlations, potentially inducing different targets; when they are instead computed from a common average correlation matrix, ˆ︂ V s depends only on the study-specific sample sizes, and this dependence is removed (Forte & Tipton, 2026). The present study adopts the latter convention in line with recent MASEM implementations (e.g., Cheung, 2015b; Ke et al., 2019). In addition, systematic associations between sample sizes and population effect sizes could introduce a similar dependence but are assumed absent in the present analyses (cf. Section 1.2). Accordingly, we do not expect substantial differences between the fixed-effect and correlation-based targets in the simulation studies.

Another potential source of target discrepancies between the fixed-effect and the correlation-based approaches concerns overidentified models. In such models, the marginal mean correlation vector may not be reproducible by a single structural parameter vector, because the model-implied correlations occupy a lower-dimensional space than m ρ . Both common-parameter approaches must then find a parameter vector that best approximates m ρ . Because their weighting structures differ, these approximations may yield slightly different parameter targets. This mechanism is expected to be weak under the conditions studied here.

2 Transparency and Openness

Additional materials, complete simulation results, simulation and analysis code for this study has been made publicly available on figshare (https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.34970254). We report all simulation conditions, parameter settings, and numbers of replications used in the simulation studies. No simulation conditions or analyses were excluded.

No new primary data were collected for this study. The empirical applications reanalyze previously published meta-analytic datasets described by Hagger et al. (2018) and Koğar and Koğar (2024). Both datasets have previously been used to illustrate specific MASEM methods (Cheung, 2022; Lohmann et al., 2026a). The present reanalyses are not replications of these earlier methodological illustrations. Instead, they use the datasets to examine a distinct methodological question by comparing the approach-specific structural parameter estimates of fixed-effect, correlation-based, and parameter-based MASEM. The current analyses were run with R version 4.4.2 and the packages rstan version 2.32.7, metaSEM version 1.5.0 and Matrix version 1.7-1.

In the preparation of this manuscript, we used ChatGPT-5.6-Sol and Claude-opus-4.6 as conversational assistants to proofread parts of the manuscript, refine English prose, discuss and check mathematical derivations, as well as review and format R code. All AI-assisted output was critically reviewed, verified, and revised by the authors, who take full responsibility for the content of the manuscript.

3 Simulation Studies

This section reports three simulation studies examining the theoretical expectations developed above. The first simulation considers a meta-analytic path model, the second a meta-analytic CFA, and the third the same CFA with nonlinear parameter transformations. Across the three simulation studies, we compare estimates of the population-level structural parameters obtained from the parameter-based, correlation-based, and fixed-effect one-stage MASEM estimators. By varying the number of primary studies as well as the magnitude and structure of between-study heterogeneity, we examine finite-sample performance and whether differences among the approaches diminish or persist as the number of studies increases.

All three one-stage MASEM approaches were implemented in Stan (Carpenter et al., 2017; Stan Development Team, 2025). Weakly informative normal priors were assigned to the population-level structural parameters, and weakly informative half-Cauchy priors were assigned to the random-effect standard deviations. The random-effects correlation matrices in the correlation-based and parameter-based models were assigned Lewandowski-Kurowicka-Joe priors (Lewandowski et al., 2009). The exact prior specifications are reported in the additional online materials (https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.34970254). Code for data generation and model estimation is also available from our accompanying repository. In all three simulation studies, we analyze posterior means as Bayesian point estimates. As our target evaluation measure, we calculate the mean deviation from the parameter-based reference target for each estimator and model parameter in each simulation condition. For the correctly specified parameter-based approach, this measure corresponds to conventional empirical bias (e.g., Muthén & Muthén, 2002). For the fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches, it may reflect both finite-sample bias and a difference between the approach-specific asymptotic target and the parameter-based reference target.

Concrete numerical predictions based on the Taylor approximations described in Section 1.2 can be derived using the parameter values set within each simulation study.

R-code to calculate these predictions can be found on our repository. Next, we will present the methods, numerical predictions, and results of each simulation study separately.

Afterwards, we evaluate our findings regarding our expectations derived above in a joint discussion section.

3.1 Simulation Study 1: Meta-Analytic Path Model

3.1.1 Method

The data-generating model was a parameter-based meta-analytic path model with two predictors and two outcomes (Figure 1). All variables were standardized. The model was just identified, with six unique correlations and six structural parameters. Accordingly, the mean structural parameter vector θ PB contained four regression coefficients-in the following referred to as β 11,PB , β 22,PB , β 12,PB , β 21,PB -, the correlation between the two predictors ϕ x,PB , and the residual covariance between the two outcomes ψ PB . The mean regression coefficients were set to β 11,PB = 0.40, β 22,PB = 0.30, β 12,PB = 0.20, and β 21,PB = 0.10. The mean predictor correlation was set to ϕ PB = 0.30, and the mean residual covariance was set to ψ PB = 0.15.

We systematically varied three design factors. First, the random-effects structure comprised a single-random-effect condition, in which only the residual covariance ψ s varied across studies, and a full-random-effects condition, in which all six structural parameters varied across studies. In the latter condition, T θ was a 6 × 6 covariance matrix. Second, we varied the magnitude of heterogeneity by setting the random-effect standard deviations (RE SD ) to either 0.05 or 0.10. In the full-random-effects condition, the off-diagonal elements of the random-effects correlation matrix were drawn from a uniform distribution ranging from 0.20 to 0.70, subject to the resulting matrix being positive definite. The resulting correlation matrix was held constant across Monte Carlo replications. Third, the number of primary studies was varied across conditions and set to S = 20, 50, 100, or 500.

The three design factors were fully crossed, resulting in 2 × 2 ×4 = 16 simulation conditions.

In the data generation, for each primary study s, the study-specific structural parameter vector θ s was generated from the parameter-based random-effects distribution and substituted into the path model to obtain the study-specific population correlation matrix R s , with ρ(θ s ) = vechs(R s ). The study-specific sample size n s was drawn from a truncated lognormal distribution with a lower bound of 200, where the logarithm of the values was normally distributed with mean 5.75 and standard deviation 0.50. Based on ρ(θ P B ) and n s , the sampling covariance matrix V s was calculated (Olkin & Siotani, 1976) and the observed correlation vector was generated as r s ∼ N (ρ (θ s ) , V s ).

For each simulation condition and model, we ran 1000 replications.

3.1.2 Bias Predictions Based on Taylor Approximations

In the single-random-effect condition, only the residual covariance ψ s varies across studies. Because ψ s enters the model-implied correlations linearly, the mean-correlation discrepancy is zero and all three approaches should produce equivalent estimates.

In the full-random-effects condition, correlated heterogeneity across multiple structural parameters introduces curvature through products of regression coefficients and predictor correlations. The Taylor approximation predicts small but nonzero target discrepancies. Under RE SD = 0.05, the largest predicted discrepancy is 0.002 for the residual covariance, with discrepancies for the regression coefficients not exceeding 0.001.

Under RE SD = 0.10, the predicted discrepancy for the residual covariance increases to 0.006 and the largest regression coefficient discrepancy to 0.004. The predictor correlation enters the relevant model-implied correlations linearly and is predicted to show no target discrepancy.

3.1.3 Results

Table 1 presents the mean deviations for S = 20 and S = 500. The complete results for S = 50 and S = 100 are reported in the additional materials in our repository. In the single-random-effect conditions, mean deviations were negligible across all three approaches, consistent with the prediction that unidimensional heterogeneity in a linearly entering parameter produces no target discrepancy.

In the full-random-effects conditions, the parameter-based approach continued to show negligible deviations (at most 0.002 at S = 20, and zero to three decimal places at S = 500), reflecting its correct specification. The correlation-based and fixed-effect approaches showed small but persistent deviations that closely matched the Taylor predictions. At S = 500, the largest deviation concerned the residual covariance (0.002 at RE SD = 0.05, and 0.006 at RE SD = 0.10), compared with predicted values of 0.002 and 0.006, respectively. The regression coefficient deviations did not exceed 0.004, and the predictor correlation showed no discernible deviation-again consistent with its linear role in the model-implied correlations. As predicted, the deviations approximately quadrupled when RE SD was doubled from 0.05 to 0.10, consistent with the quadratic scaling predicted by the Taylor approximation (Equation 15). Furthermore, the fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches produced identical deviations at S = 500 as expected.

Across all conditions, Monte Carlo standard errors did not exceed 0.001 for the mean deviations and 0.0004 for the paired differences between approaches.

3.2 Simulation Study 2: Meta-analytic Confirmatory Factor Analysis

3.2.1 Method

The data-generating model was a meta-analytic CFA with two latent factors and three indicators per latent factor. All variables were again standardized. Thus, the vector θ PB contained seven mean parameters, namely six standardized factor loadings and a latent factor correlation. The loadings were set to λ 1,PB = 0.75, λ 2,PB = 0.70, λ 3,PB = 0.60, λ 4,PB = 0.75, λ 5,PB = 0.70, and λ 6,PB = 0.60.

The mean factor correlation was varied as a design factor.

In the second simulation study, we systematically varied four design factors. First, the mean latent factor correlation ϕ PB was set to either 0.30 or 0.80. Second, we again varied the random-effects structure. In the single-random-effect condition, only the latent factor correlation ϕ s varied across studies. In the full-random-effects condition, all six factor loadings and the latent factor correlation varied across studies, such that T θ was a 7 × 7 covariance matrix. Third, RE SD was set to either 0.05 or 0.10. Finally, the number of primary studies was set to S = 20, 50, 100, or 500. The four design factors were fully crossed, resulting in 2 × 2 × 2 ×4 = 32 simulation conditions.

Because normally distributed random effects on the original parameter scale could produce standardized loadings or factor correlations exceeding their admissible upper bound, generated values greater than 1 were replaced by 0.99 before constructing the study-specific correlation matrices.

Study-specific sample sizes as well as population and observed correlation vectors were generated following the same procedure as in Simulation Study 1. For each simulation condition and model, we ran 1000 replications.

3.2.2 Bias Predictions Based on Taylor Approximations

The CFA model yields 15 unique correlations and 7 structural parameters. As in Simulation Study 1, the single-random-effect condition involves heterogeneity in only the factor correlation, which enters the model-implied correlations linearly when the loadings are fixed. All three approaches are therefore predicted to produce equivalent estimates.

In the full-random-effects condition, correlated loading heterogeneity introduces curvature through products of loadings and, for cross-factor correlations, products of loadings with the factor correlation. The Taylor approximation predicts that the largest discrepancy occurs for the factor correlation, followed by the second loading λ 2 . Under a random-effect standard deviation of 0.05, the predicted discrepancies are approximately 0.004 for the factor correlation and 0.002 for λ 2 . Under a random-effect standard deviation of 0.10, these increase to approximately 0.014 and 0.008, respectively, consistent with quadratic scaling.

3.2.3 Results

Table 2 presents the mean deviations for S = 20 and S = 500. The complete results for S = 50 and S = 100 are reported in the online materials.

In the single-random-effect conditions, mean deviations were negligible across all three approaches and both levels of ϕ PB , consistent with the prediction that unidimensional heterogeneity in the linearly entering factor correlation produces no target discrepancy. A small negative deviation of the factor correlation (up to -0.003) appeared at S = 20 under RE SD = 0.10 but vanished at S = 500, indicating a finite-sample effect.

In the full-random-effects conditions, the parameter-based approach showed negligible deviations across all conditions, confirming its correct specification. The common-parameter approaches showed persistent deviations that closely matched the Taylor predictions. At S = 500 and RE SD = 0.10, the largest deviation occurred for the factor correlation (0.013-0.014 across ϕ PB conditions), compared with predicted values of approximately 0.014. The second-largest deviations concerned λ 2 (0.006-0.007), again consistent with the predicted value of approximately 0.008. Deviations approximately quadrupled from RE SD = 0.05 to RE SD = 0.10, again consistent with the quadratic scaling predicted by the Taylor approximation. The fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches produced nearly identical deviation patterns, with differences not exceeding 0.001 at S = 500, as expected.

The full-random-effects pattern remained virtually unchanged between S = 20 and S = 500, confirming that the observed deviations reflect persistent target discrepancies rather than finite-sample effects. Across all conditions, MCSE did not exceed 0.001 for the mean deviations and 0.0004 for the paired differences between approaches.

3.3 Simulation Study 3: Meta-analytic Confirmatory Factor Analysis with

Nonlinear Transformations

3.3.1 Method

Simulation Study 3 used the same overidentified two-factor CFA and the same general simulation design as Simulation Study 2. In contrast to Simulation Study 2, however, the parameter random effects were introduced on unconstrained scales. The resulting study-specific parameters were subsequently transformed to their admissible ranges. This avoided the need for the boundary adjustment used in Simulation Study 2.

For the standardized factor loadings, let η λ j ,s denote the study-specific parameter on the unconstrained scale. The corresponding loading was obtained using the inverse-logit transformation, which restricts the loadings to the interval (0, 1). The location parameters on the unconstrained scale were set to η λ 1 ,PB = η λ 4 ,PB = 1.20, η λ 2 ,PB = η λ 5 ,PB = 0.90, and η λ 3 ,PB = η λ 6 ,PB = 0.60, yielding loading magnitudes broadly comparable to those in Simulation Study 2 after back-transformation.

The latent factor correlation was parameterized on the Fisher-z scale, and the population mean was set to the values 0.30 and 1.10 in the simulation design. The Fisher-z transformation restricted the study-specific factor correlations to the interval (-1, 1).

Mapped back to the correlation scale using the inverse Fisher-z transformation, the population means broadly align with the values set in Simulation 2.

Because the nonlinear transformations change the magnitude of between-study variation, the random-effect standard deviations on the unconstrained scales are not directly comparable to those used in Simulation Study 2. However, we calibrated the unconstrained-scale RE SD in preliminary simulations such that the resulting standard deviations of the back-transformed study-specific parameters were approximately 0.05 and 0.10 on their original scales. Because the effect of each transformation depends on the parameter location, the required unconstrained RE SD differed across parameters and simulation conditions. The exact values are documented in the online material.

The same inverse-logit and inverse Fisher-z transformations were implemented in all three fitted MASEM models. Mean deviations were evaluated on the unconstrained parameter scale. The reported deviations therefore refer to the logit scale for the factor loadings and the Fisher-z scale for the latent factor correlation, rather than to the original parameter scales.

3.3.2 Bias Predictions Based on Taylor Approximations

Simulation In the full-random-effects condition, the inverse-logit transformation of the loadings introduces substantial additional curvature. The Taylor approximation predicts discrepancies for the loading parameters that are considerably larger than in Simulation Study 2. Under RE SD = 0.10, the predicted discrepancies for η λ 1 and η λ 4 reach approximately -0.10 and -0.07, respectively.

3.3.3 Results

Table 3 presents the mean deviations for S = 20 and S = 500 on the unconstrained parameter scales (logit for factor loadings, Fisher z for the factor correlation). Results for S = 50 and S = 100 are reported in the online materials.

In the single-random-effect conditions, loading deviations were negligible across all approaches, consistent with the absence of loading heterogeneity. Deviations of the factor-correlation parameter were also small except when ζ PB = 1.10 and RE SD = 0.10, where the common-parameter approaches showed persistent deviations of approximately -0.009 to -0.010 at S = 500, closely matching the predicted value of -0.010. This confirms that the nonlinear inverse Fisher z transformation produces a target discrepancy even under unidimensional heterogeneity, as predicted by the theoretical analysis. The smaller discrepancy at ζ PB = 0.30 (-0.004 predicted, -0.004 observed) is also consistent with the reduced curvature near the origin.

In the full-random-effects conditions, substantially larger deviations emerged for the loading parameters, reflecting the additional curvature introduced by the inverse-logit transformation. At S = 500 and RE SD = 0.10, the largest deviations of the common-parameter approaches occurred for η λ 1 and η λ 4 (approximately -0.08 to -0.09), consistent with the predicted values of approximately -0.10 and -0.07. Deviations for the remaining loading parameters ranged from -0.017 to -0.035, and deviations approximately quadrupled from RE SD = 0.05 to RE SD = 0.10, consistent with the predicted quadratic scaling. The parameter-based approach showed substantially smaller deviations that did not exceed 0.003 at S = 500, reflecting its correct specification. The factor-correlation deviations remained comparatively small (at most 0.011), consistent with the factor correlation entering the model-implied correlations through a less curved mapping than the inverse-logit transformation.

Unlike in the previous simulation studies, the parameter-based approach showed noticeable deviations at S = 20 under RE SD = 0.10 (up to 0.044). These deviations largely disappeared at S = 500, indicating finite-sample effects rather than target discrepancies.

Furthermore, the fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches produced similar deviation patterns at S = 500, with differences generally not exceeding 0.002. This is consistent with the expectation that their asymptotic targets are approximately equal under the implementations employed.

Across all conditions, MCSE did not exceed 0.005 for the mean deviations and 0.002 for the paired differences. Under S = 500, MCSE were consistently below 0.001 for the mean deviations and below 0.0005 for paired differences.

3.4 Summary of the Simulation Findings

Taken together, the three simulation studies supported the theoretical expectation that the location of the random effects in MASEM approaches can affect the asymptotic targets of structural parameters, even in relatively simple path models and CFA models, such as those frequently used in MASEM studies. Across all three studies, moving from a single random effect to multidimensional, correlated parameter heterogeneity either introduced or amplified persistent deviations of the fixed-effect and correlation-based estimates from the parameter-based reference values. These deviations were generally larger under stronger heterogeneity. This pattern is consistent with the order-of-averaging argument presented above: When several structural parameters vary jointly across studies, products and cross-products of these parameters can result in a marginal mean correlation vector that differs from the correlation vector implied by the average model parameters across studies. Importantly, this occurred even though the study-specific structural models were correctly specified and are conventionally classified as linear SEMs.

Across all three studies, the Taylor approximations provided accurate predictions of the observed target discrepancies between the parameter-based and the common-parameter approaches. In Simulation Studies 1 and 2, predicted and observed values agreed to three decimal places for most parameters. In Simulation Study 3, the predictions remained qualitatively accurate but showed somewhat larger deviations from the observed values under RE SD = 0.10, which is likely due to the contribution of higher-order terms not captured by the second-order Taylor approximation. Nevertheless, the approximations correctly predicted the relative magnitude, direction, and scaling of the discrepancies across all conditions.

Comparing Simulation Studies 1 and 2 shows that both just-identified and overidentified structural models can produce discrepancies between the parameter-based and common-parameter targets when multidimensional, correlated heterogeneity is present.

In both studies, the single-random-effect conditions produced negligible discrepancies because the heterogeneous parameter entered the model-implied correlations linearly, eliminating the curvature required for a mean-correlation discrepancy.

The comparison between Simulation Studies 2 and 3 illustrates the additional consequences of nonlinear parameter transformations. When the factor correlation varied on the Fisher z scale rather than the original scale, persistent discrepancies emerged even under unidimensional heterogeneity-confirming that nonlinear transformations can be a sufficient source of curvature. Under multidimensional heterogeneity, the inverse-logit transformation of the loadings produced substantially larger discrepancies than those observed in Simulation Study 2, particularly for parameters with greater curvature in the transformation function.

Furthermore, varying the number of primary studies helped distinguish finite-sample effects from persistent target discrepancies. Deviations of the parameter-based estimates in the more complex conditions of Simulation Study 3 largely disappeared as S increased, whereas several deviations of the fixed-effect and correlation-based estimates remained at S = 500. This persistence is consistent with the fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches converging to targets that differ from the parameter-based reference, rather than with finite-sample bias alone.

Differences between the fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches were generally small across all three simulation studies. In Simulation Studies 1 and 2, the two approaches produced virtually identical deviations at S = 500. In Simulation Study 3, small but persistent differences emerged under full random effects, with the largest paired differences reaching approximately 0.002 at S = 500. These differences were much smaller than those between the parameter-based and common-parameter approaches but were considerably different from zero based on their Monte Carlo standard errors. The projection mechanism described in Section 1.2.5 offers one possible explanation: when the mean-correlation discrepancy cannot be exactly reproduced by a single parameter vector, approach-specific weighting may lead to slightly different approximations of the marginal mean vector.

However, the present simulation design was not specifically constructed to isolate this mechanism, and further research is needed in this respect.

4 Empirical Examples

We illustrate the three one-stage MASEM approaches using two published meta-analytic datasets. Reflecting the two model types considered in the simulations, we reanalyze a meta-analytic path model and a meta-analytic confirmatory factor analysis.

Analysis and model code are available in the online material (https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.34970254).

4.1 Meta-Analytic Path Model: Reanalysis of Hagger et al. (2018)

Hagger et al. (2018) extended the meta-analysis of the reasoned action approach by McEachan et al. (2016) to examine the role of past behavior. The underlying primary studies applied the reasoned action approach to a broad range of health-promoting and health-compromising behaviors, including physical activity, healthy eating, screening attendance, smoking, and drug use. The resulting dataset was also used by Cheung (2022) to illustrate MASEM for mediation models. For the present reanalysis, we focused on past behavior, behavioral intention, and subsequent behavior. We fitted a simple mediation model in which past behavior predicted subsequent behavior indirectly through intention (ab) and directly through path c ′ . Of the 81 available correlation matrices, 79 contained sufficient information for this model, with missing correlations handled using the full-information likelihood (Jak & Cheung, 2020).

As shown in Table 4, the three approaches produced consistently very similar point estimates for target population-level SEM parameters. However, two small differences were apparent: the fixed-effect estimate of path a was somewhat larger (0.57 compared with 0.53 and 0.54), whereas path c ′ was slightly larger for the correlation-based and fixed-effect approaches (0.40 and 0.41) than for the parameter-based approach (0.38). The latter difference was also reflected in the estimated total effects (0.53 and 0.54 versus 0.51).

In addition, the fixed-effect approach yielded substantially smaller posterior standard deviations and narrower credible intervals. Given the substantial between-study heterogeneity, however, these posterior standard deviations are likely underestimated because the fixed-effect model does not account for heterogeneity beyond sampling error.

Fixed-effect MASEM may alternatively be combined with resampling-based standard-error estimation, as demonstrated in previous MASEM applications (Lohmann et al., 2026a).

However, the core focus of the present investigation was on comparing the mean SEM parameter estimates.

4.2 Meta-Analytic Confirmatory Factor Analysis: Reanalysis of Kogar &

Kogar (2024) Koğar and Koğar (2024) examined the factor structure of the 12-item Multidimensional Scale of Perceived Social Support (MSPSS) using 59 samples from 54 primary studies and found support for a correlated three-factor model comprising the latent factors support from significant others, family, and friends. We reestimated the three-factor model using all 59 correlation matrices, with missing correlations handled through the full-information likelihood.

As shown in Table 5, the three approaches produced again highly similar factor-loading estimates, which ranged from .77 to .88 and differed by no more than .02 across approaches. Slightly larger differences emerged for the factor correlations. The correlation-based estimate for the association between family and friends was somewhat lower (.49) than the parameter-based and fixed-effect estimates (both .52), whereas the fixed-effect estimates of the two correlations involving significant others were slightly higher (.62 and .63) than the corresponding parameter-and correlation-based estimates (.59 and .61). The parameter-based approach yielded somewhat wider credible intervals for the factor correlations, whereas the fixed-effect approach produced the narrowest intervals for both the factor loadings and correlations. As discussed in the preceding example, the fixed-effect approach underestimates uncertainty because it does not account for between-study heterogeneity. This limitation can be addressed by combining fixed-effect estimation with alternative uncertainty-estimation procedures, such as resampling-based standard errors.

5 Overall Discussion

In this study, we compared the correlation-based, parameter-based, and fixed-effect approaches to MASEM with respect to their approach-specific population estimands. Our theoretical derivations showed that the three approaches can target systematically different quantities in common MASEM applications, including meta-analytic path models and CFAs. In particular, the derivations identified the joint heterogeneity of multiple SEM parameters and covariance among their random effects as one mechanism that can produce target differences. Consistent with this mechanism and bias predictions based on the second-order Taylor approximation, persistent differences emerged in simulation conditions that combined multidimensional heterogeneity with correlated random effects and generally increased with the magnitude of heterogeneity. Moreover, nonlinear parameter transformations, which are commonly used to keep parameters within their admissible ranges, produced target differences even under unidimensional heterogeneity. The magnitude of these differences depended on the heterogeneity and the local curvature of the transformation. Across all simulation studies, the fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches produced largely equivalent results. Small but statistically distinguishable differences between the two emerged only in Simulation Study 3 under the combination of nonlinear transformations, overidentification, and strong multidimensional heterogeneity, and even in this case they were considerably smaller than the discrepancies relative to the parameter-based approach. However, the present simulation studies were not designed to systematically isolate the mechanisms driving differences between the fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches.

The two empirical examples further illustrated that the choice of MASEM approach can affect the resulting structural parameter estimates. Although the estimates were generally similar, small differences emerged for individual path coefficients, item loadings, and latent factor correlations. However, neither our theoretical considerations nor the simulation studies and empirical examples establish which approach is generally most appropriate. Instead, the choice depends on the substantive estimand: Is the primary aim to estimate the SEM parameter vector that reproduces-or best approximates-the mean correlation structure across primary studies, or to investigate the mean and variance of the distribution of study-specific SEM parameters? The estimand of a MASEM application should therefore be defined explicitly before selecting an analytic approach.

The parameter-based approach may be particularly suitable when researchers are interested in the average and the heterogeneity of SEM parameters across primary studies.

It also permits the calculation of prediction intervals for SEM parameters in a new study and extends naturally to moderator analyses, including the quantification of explained parameter heterogeneity (see Ke et al., 2025). The correlation-based approach may be preferred when the mean correlation structure across primary studies is of central substantive interest and constitutes the starting point of the meta-analytic investigation.

By contrast, fixed-effect MASEM assumes that between-study variation beyond sampling error can be ignored. This assumption may be implausible in many psychological applications and can lead to uncertainty estimates that are too small, as was also suggested by our empirical examples. Nevertheless, it can provide a useful working model and may be combined with cluster-robust uncertainty-estimation, such as resampling, to support valid inference under between-study heterogeneity (Lohmann et al., 2026a).

5.1 Limitations and Future Research

Our findings should be considered in light of several limitations that also provide directions for future research. First, an important issue concerns the dimensionality and specification of the random-effects covariance structure. Meta-analytic CFA applications involving several dozen items are not uncommon (e.g., Raimondi et al., 2023; Yeung et al., 2020), resulting in a large number of unique input correlations. The number of heterogeneity variances and covariances in the correlation-based approach therefore increases considerably faster than the number of random-effects parameters in a parameter-based CFA. Nevertheless, an unstructured random-effects covariance matrix can also become difficult to estimate in parameter-based MASEM, particularly when the number of primary studies is limited. More parsimonious alternatives include estimating only a diagonal covariance matrix, imposing an equicorrelated structure with parameter-specific heterogeneity variances but a single common random-effects correlation (e.g., Jak & Cheung, 2026; Ke et al., 2019; Lohmann et al., 2026b), or regularizing the covariance parameters toward zero. Future research should compare the statistical and computational performance of these structures and examine their robustness when the assumed random-effects structure is misspecified.

Second, the simulations considered a limited set of data-generating conditions and assumed both a correctly specified structural model and normally distributed random effects. Future studies could examine the consequences of model misspecification and skewed or otherwise nonnormal random-effects distributions. Moreover, because the present data were generated from parameter-based models, complementary simulations could consider correlation-based data-generating mechanisms in which heterogeneity is introduced directly at the level of the correlations or hybrid mechanisms combining both sources of heterogeneity.

Third, the simulations did not directly manipulate conditions under which the weighting schemes of the fixed-effect and correlation-based approaches may result in different asymptotic targets for the structural parameters. In particular, primary-study sample sizes were generated independently of the study-specific structural parameters (and correlations), potentially limiting weighting-dependent discrepancies (see, e.g., Forte & Tipton, 2026; Hafdahl, 2008). Future research could examine informative-precision settings in which study size covaries with population correlations and thereby more directly isolate the interaction between weighting and model identification.

Finally, the two empirical examples provide only an initial indication of the differences that may arise in applied analyses. Further comparisons across a broader range of real meta-analytic datasets and structural models are needed to assess the frequency and practical magnitude of approach-specific differences.

5.2 Conclusion

Our theoretical derivations, simulation studies, and empirical examples show that MASEM approaches can target different population quantities, depending on where between-study heterogeneity is represented in the model. Differences between the parameter-based approach and the common-parameter approaches are particularly plausible under multidimensional, correlated heterogeneity or nonlinear parameter transformations and tend to increase with the magnitude of heterogeneity. Researchers applying MASEM should consider whether their primary interest lies in a common SEM parameter vector representing the marginal correlation structure or in the mean of the study-specific SEM parameters.

S = number of studies; RE = random effect condition; ϕ PB = latent correlation condition; Param. = parameter; RE SD magnitude of between-study heterogeneity; Param. = parameter; PB = Parameter-based MASEM; CB = Correlation-based MASEM; FE = Fixed-effect MASEM.

concavity of the inverse Fisher z transformation. Because the indicator correlations are linear in ϕ s when all loadings are fixed, this shift can be absorbed by a common factor correlation equal to E(ϕ s ), whose value on the Fisher z scale is ζ * A = atanh[E(ϕ s )], yielding the second expression. The common-parameter target is therefore shifted toward zero relative to ζ PB , with the magnitude of the shift increasing with τ² ζ . When ϕ0 = 0, the discrepancy vanishes because the transformation has zero curvature at the origin.

Study 3 uses the same CFA model as Simulation Study 2 but introduces random effects on unconstrained scales, adding curvature from the inverse-logit and inverse Fisher z transformations. In the single-random-effect condition, the factor correlation varies on the Fisher z scale. Unlike in Simulation Study 2, this produces a nonzero mean-correlation discrepancy because the heterogeneous parameter now enters the model-implied correlations through the nonlinear inverse Fisher z transformation. The predicted target discrepancy on the Fisher z scale is -0.001 under RE SD of 0.05 and -0.004 under 0.10, with the shift directed toward zero. The discrepancy is larger when the mean factor correlation is ζ PB = 1.10 (-0.002 and -0.010) than when it is ζ PB = 0.30 (-0.001 and -0.004), reflecting greater curvature.

Note. M = posterior mean; SD = posterior standard deviation; CI = Bayesian credible interval. a = Past behavior → Intention; b = Intention → Behavior; c ′ = direct effect (Past behavior → Behavior).

Figure 1Schematic representation of the parameter-based meta-analytic path model in Simulation Study 1. The lower panel shows the study-specific path model, whereas the upper panel represents the means and between-study variances of its structural parameters. In the single-random-effect condition, only ψ s varied across studies; in the full-random-effects condition, all six parameters varied and their random effects were correlated, as depicted.
